Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, thank you. What brought me in is… it’s this checking I keep doing. I’ll be at home, getting ready to leave or trying to finish something, and I find myself checking the same boxes, the same little lists, the drawers in the condo—sometimes more than once, more than twice, really—before I can let myself move on.

And the frustrating part is I know I already looked. I know it. It’s not as if I’m confused about whether I opened the drawer or checked the list. I can remember doing it, but then I feel like I have to look again anyway, just to be sure, and then sometimes that still doesn’t feel like enough. My wife will be standing there waiting for me, and I can tell she’s thinking, “Just leave it, we need to go,” and I’m thinking the same thing, frankly. But I still get pulled back to check one more time. It’s become bothersome enough that I thought I should talk to someone about it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do. It’s not like a voice or anything like that—it’s more of a nagging thought that keeps circling back. I’ll think, “What if I threw away something important from my old teaching files?” Or, “What if I put a tool I’m going to need into the wrong box?” And I can tell myself, very reasonably, “No, that probably didn’t happen,” or “You would have noticed,” but it doesn’t quite put the matter to rest.

It’s frustrating because the thought has a kind of stickiness to it. I can be trying to move on with my day, and then it pops back in—maybe I discarded some paper I shouldn’t have, or maybe that one tool is not where it ought to be. Even when I know it’s unlikely, it makes me unsettled, like I can’t quite relax until I’ve reassured myself somehow.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I suppose in a way, yes—but it’s not always some dramatic catastrophe I’m imagining. It’s more that I feel I can’t move on until I’ve made sure nothing has been missed or put in the wrong place. So I’ll check the same boxes, the same lists, or those condo drawers again and again, even though I know perfectly well I already looked.

My wife will be there waiting, and I can see she wants me to just leave it be, and honestly I want to leave it be too. But there’s this uncomfortable feeling that if I don’t check one more time, I might have overlooked something important. So it’s less like I truly believe something terrible is definitely going to happen, and more like I’m trying to quiet that worry enough to be able to walk away.

Hmm… symmetry or a specific order? I don’t think so, no—not in the way I think you mean.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I mean, I like things done properly, sure—probably most people do—but I don’t know that I have a general need for perfection, like everything has to be flawless or arranged exactly so.

What bothers me is more the uncertainty afterward. If I’ve done something and then I start wondering whether I did it correctly, or whether I missed a step, that can bother me. But it’s not so much that I’m standing there thinking, “This has to be perfect.” It’s more like, “Did I actually do what I meant to do?” or “Am I going to regret not making sure?” So… I’d say not perfection in the strict sense. More just difficulty letting it feel settled.

So it’s less like I truly believe something terrible is definitely going to happen, and more like I’m trying to quiet that worry enough to be able to walk away.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself checking to make sure things are symmetrical or in a specific order?\n\nPatient: Hmm… symmetry or a specific order? I don’t think so, no—not in the way I think you mean.\n\nI’m not usually looking at something and feeling it has to be perfectly lined up, or that things have to be arranged just so before I can leave it alone. It’s less about whether the objects look right or are balanced, and more… well, I suppose I’m checking whether I missed something. But if you’re asking specifically about symmetry, like making sure items are even or in a particular pattern, I haven’t really noticed that with myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to do things perfectly, or that if something is not done just right, it will bother you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I mean, I like things done properly, sure—probably most people do—but I don’t know that I have a general need for perfection, like everything has to be flawless or arranged exactly so.\n\nWhat bothers me is more the uncertainty afterward. If I’ve done something and then I start wondering whether I did it correctly, or whether I missed a step, that can bother me. But it’s not so much that I’m standing there thinking, “This has to be perfect.” It’s more like, “Did I actually do what I meant to do?” or “Am I going to regret not making sure?” So… I’d say not perfection in the strict sense. More just difficulty letting it feel settled.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do—though I wouldn’t say it’s a constant panic or anything dramatic like that. It’s more of a nagging worry that starts up when I think I may have made some small mistake that could matter later.

For example, with my old teaching files, I’ll get this thought that maybe I threw away something important. Even if I can tell myself, “Well, you looked through that already,” or “It’s probably not in there,” the thought doesn’t really disappear. It sort of comes back around, and then I feel unsettled.

Or with tools and boxes, I might think I put a tool in the wrong box, or that something I’ll need later has ended up somewhere it shouldn’t be. And I know, rationally, that it’s unlikely, or that I could probably sort it out later if I had to. But that doesn’t always help in the moment. The worry keeps poking at me, like I can’t quite trust that I’ve done it correctly, and then it’s hard to just leave it alone and feel finished with it.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, thank you. What brought me in is… it’s this checking I keep doing. I’ll be at home, getting ready to leave or trying to finish something, and I find myself checking the same boxes, the same little lists, the drawers in the condo—sometimes more than once, more than twice, really—before I can let myself move on.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I know I already looked. I know it. It’s not as if I’m confused about whether I opened the drawer or checked the list. I can remember doing it, but then I feel like I have to look again anyway, just to be sure, and then sometimes that still doesn’t feel like enough. My wife will be standing there waiting for me, and I can tell she’s thinking, “Just leave it, we need to go,” and I’m thinking the same thing, frankly. But I still get pulled back to check one more time. It’s become bothersome enough that I thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any disturbing or intrusive thoughts that keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not like a voice or anything like that—it’s more of a nagging thought that keeps circling back. I can be trying to move on with my day, and then it pops back in—maybe I discarded some paper I shouldn’t have, or maybe that one tool is not where it ought to be. I mean, I like things done properly, sure—probably most people do—but I don’t know that I have a general need for perfection, like everything has to be flawless or arranged exactly so.\n\nWhat bothers me is more the uncertainty afterward. If I’ve done something and then I start wondering whether I did it correctly, or whether I missed a step, that can bother me. But it’s not so much that I’m standing there thinking, “This has to be perfect.” It’s more like, “Did I actually do what I meant to do?” or “Am I going to regret not making sure?” So… I’d say not perfection in the strict sense. More just difficulty letting it feel settled.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried when you think about making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do—though I wouldn’t say it’s a constant panic or anything dramatic like that. It’s more of a nagging worry that starts up when I think I may have made some small mistake that could matter later.\n\nFor example, with my old teaching files, I’ll get this thought that maybe I threw away something important. Even if I can tell myself, “Well, you looked through that already,” or “It’s probably not in there,” the thought doesn’t really disappear. It sort of comes back around, and then I feel unsettled.\n\nOr with tools and boxes, I might think I put a tool in the wrong box, or that something I’ll need later has ended up somewhere it shouldn’t be. And I know, rationally, that it’s unlikely, or that I could probably sort it out later if I had to. But that doesn’t always help in the moment. The worry keeps poking at me, like I can’t quite trust that I’ve done it correctly, and then it’s hard to just leave it alone and feel finished with it.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre your repetitive checks focused on preventing something specific from happening, or is it more about feeling a general sense of relief after checking?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 7\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more the relief than preventing one very specific thing, if I’m being honest. I mean, there are little worries attached to it — like maybe something important is in the wrong place, or I missed something in a box or a list — but it’s not usually that I’m picturing some exact disaster happening.

What brought me in, really, is that I’ll find myself checking the same boxes, the same lists, the same drawers in the condo, again and again before I can move on. And the frustrating part is I know I already looked. I can remember looking. But I still get this unsettled feeling, like it hasn’t quite registered, and I think, “Just check once more and then you’ll feel done.”

And of course my wife is standing there waiting for me to just leave it be, and I’m aware of that too, which makes me feel a bit foolish. It’s not that I think checking the drawer three times is going to prevent some clear catastrophe. It’s more that if I don’t do it, I feel unfinished, like I can’t quite walk away comfortably. Then after checking, there’s a little bit of relief — sometimes only briefly — and I can move on.

It’s not that I think checking the drawer three times is going to prevent some clear catastrophe. It’s more that if I don’t do it, I feel unfinished, like I can’t quite walk away comfortably. Then after checking, there’s a little bit of relief — sometimes only briefly — and I can move on.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're trying to regain control over a situation when you repeat these checks?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Obsessive-Compulsive Disorder, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to do things perfectly, or that if something is not done just right, it will bother you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I mean, I like things done properly, sure—probably most people do—but I don’t know that I have a general need for perfection, like everything has to be flawless or arranged exactly so.\n\nWhat bothers me is more the uncertainty afterward. If I’ve done something and then I start wondering whether I did it correctly, or whether I missed a step, that can bother me. But it’s not so much that I’m standing there thinking, “This has to be perfect.” It’s more like, “Did I actually do what I meant to do?” or “Am I going to regret not making sure?” So… I’d say not perfection in the strict sense. More just difficulty letting it feel settled.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried when you think about making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do—though I wouldn’t say it’s a constant panic or anything dramatic like that. It’s more of a nagging worry that starts up when I think I may have made some small mistake that could matter later.\n\nFor example, with my old teaching files, I’ll get this thought that maybe I threw away something important. Even if I can tell myself, “Well, you looked through that already,” or “It’s probably not in there,” the thought doesn’t really disappear. It sort of comes back around, and then I feel unsettled.\n\nOr with tools and boxes, I might think I put a tool in the wrong box, or that something I’ll need later has ended up somewhere it shouldn’t be. And I know, rationally, that it’s unlikely, or that I could probably sort it out later if I had to. But that doesn’t always help in the moment. The worry keeps poking at me, like I can’t quite trust that I’ve done it correctly, and then it’s hard to just leave it alone and feel finished with it.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive checks focused on preventing something specific from happening, or is it more about feeling a general sense of relief after checking?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the relief than preventing one very specific thing, if I’m being honest. I mean, there are little worries attached to it — like maybe something important is in the wrong place, or I missed something in a box or a list — but it’s not usually that I’m picturing some exact disaster happening.\n\nWhat brought me in, really, is that I’ll find myself checking the same boxes, the same lists, the same drawers in the condo, again and again before I can move on. And the frustrating part is I know I already looked. I can remember looking. But I still get this unsettled feeling, like it hasn’t quite registered, and I think, “Just check once more and then you’ll feel done.”\n\nAnd of course my wife is standing there waiting for me to just leave it be, and I’m aware of that too, which makes me feel a bit foolish. It’s not that I think checking the drawer three times is going to prevent some clear catastrophe. It’s more that if I don’t do it, I feel unfinished, like I can’t quite walk away comfortably. Then after checking, there’s a little bit of relief — sometimes only briefly — and I can move on.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're trying to regain control over a situation when you repeat these checks?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 76 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Compulsions\nmanifestation: \"What brought me in is that I find myself checking the same boxes, lists, and condo drawers over and over before I can move on, even though I know perfectly well I already looked and my wife is waiting for me to just leave it be.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I suppose in a way, yes, but not control in a big dramatic sense. It’s more like I’m trying to get control over that unsettled feeling in my own head.

What brought me in is that I find myself checking the same boxes, the same lists, and drawers in the condo over and over before I can move on. And the odd thing is, I know perfectly well I already looked. It’s not as if I blacked out or truly don’t remember. I can say to myself, “You opened that drawer already,” or “You went through that box.” But somehow it still doesn’t feel finished.

And then my wife is there waiting for me to just leave it be, and I’m aware of how unnecessary it must look from the outside, but I still feel pulled back to it. So yes, maybe it is a kind of trying to regain control—not of the drawer or the box exactly, but of the doubt that keeps hanging around after I’ve already checked.

It’s more that I feel I can’t move on until I’ve made sure nothing has been missed or put in the wrong place. It’s not that I think checking the drawer three times is going to prevent some clear catastrophe. It’s more that if I don’t do it, I feel unfinished, like I can’t quite walk away comfortably. Then after checking, there’s a little bit of relief — sometimes only briefly — and I can move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to regain control over a situation when you repeat these checks?\n\nPatient: I suppose in a way, yes, but not control in a big dramatic sense. It’s more like I’m trying to get control over that unsettled feeling in my own head.\n\nWhat brought me in is that I find myself checking the same boxes, the same lists, and drawers in the condo over and over before I can move on. And the odd thing is, I know perfectly well I already looked. It’s not as if I blacked out or truly don’t remember. I can say to myself, “You opened that drawer already,” or “You went through that box.” But somehow it still doesn’t feel finished.\n\nAnd then my wife is there waiting for me to just leave it be, and I’m aware of how unnecessary it must look from the outside, but I still feel pulled back to it. So yes, maybe it is a kind of trying to regain control—not of the drawer or the box exactly, but of the doubt that keeps hanging around after I’ve already checked.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your checking is more about avoiding making a mistake or making sure everything is okay?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s probably both, but if I had to describe it, it’s less that I’m preventing some big obvious mistake and more that I’m trying to make sure things are okay before I can let go of it.

What brought me in is really this pattern where I’ll check the same boxes, the same lists, the same drawers in the condo, and then somehow I’m back there checking them again. And the strange part is I know I already looked. I can remember opening the drawer or going through the box. It’s not as though I genuinely have no idea whether I did it. But it doesn’t feel settled in my mind.

So I’ll think, “Well, just look one more time and then you’ll know it’s okay.” And sometimes my wife is waiting for me to just stop and move on, and I’m very aware of that, which makes me feel a bit embarrassed. But in the moment it feels hard to just walk away, because then I’m left with that feeling that maybe I missed something or didn’t do it properly. So yes, I suppose it’s about avoiding a mistake in a small way, but mainly it’s about needing that sense that everything is okay before I can move on.

I know it. It’s not as if I’m confused about whether I opened the drawer or checked the list. I can remember doing it, but then I feel like I have to look again anyway, just to be sure, and then sometimes that still doesn’t feel like enough. My wife will be standing there waiting for me, and I can tell she’s thinking, “Just leave it, we need to go,” and I’m thinking the same thing, frankly. But I still get pulled back to check one more time. It’s become bothersome enough that I thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any disturbing or intrusive thoughts that keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not like a voice or anything like that—it’s more of a nagging thought that keeps circling back. I’ll think, “What if I threw away something important from my old teaching files?” Or, “What if I put a tool I’m going to need into the wrong box?” And I can tell myself, very reasonably, “No, that probably didn’t happen,” or “You would have noticed,” but it doesn’t quite put the matter to rest.\n\nIt’s frustrating because the thought has a kind of stickiness to it. I can be trying to move on with my day, and then it pops back in—maybe I discarded some paper I shouldn’t have, or maybe that one tool is not where it ought to be. Even when I know it’s unlikely, it makes me unsettled, like I can’t quite relax until I’ve reassured myself somehow.\n\nDoctor: Are you checking these things because you're trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I suppose in a way, yes—but it’s not always some dramatic catastrophe I’m imagining. It’s more that I feel I can’t move on until I’ve made sure nothing has been missed or put in the wrong place. So I’ll check the same boxes, the same lists, or those condo drawers again and again, even though I know perfectly well I already looked.\n\nMy wife will be there waiting, and I can see she wants me to just leave it be, and honestly I want to leave it be too. But there’s this uncomfortable feeling that if I don’t check one more time, I might have overlooked something important. So it’s less like I truly believe something terrible is definitely going to happen, and more like I’m trying to quiet that worry enough to be able to walk away.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself checking to make sure things are symmetrical or in a specific order?\n\nPatient: Hmm… symmetry or a specific order? I don’t think so, no—not in the way I think you mean.\n\nI’m not usually looking at something and feeling it has to be perfectly lined up, or that things have to be arranged just so before I can leave it alone. It’s less about whether the objects look right or are balanced, and more… well, I suppose I’m checking whether I missed something. But if you’re asking specifically about symmetry, like making sure items are even or in a particular pattern, I haven’t really noticed that with myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to do things perfectly, or that if something is not done just right, it will bother you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I mean, I like things done properly, sure—probably most people do—but I don’t know that I have a general need for perfection, like everything has to be flawless or arranged exactly so.\n\nWhat bothers me is more the uncertainty afterward. If I’ve done something and then I start wondering whether I did it correctly, or whether I missed a step, that can bother me. But it’s not so much that I’m standing there thinking, “This has to be perfect.” It’s more like, “Did I actually do what I meant to do?” or “Am I going to regret not making sure?” So… I’d say not perfection in the strict sense. More just difficulty letting it feel settled.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried when you think about making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do—though I wouldn’t say it’s a constant panic or anything dramatic like that. You are about\nto talk with the doctor.\n\nLooking at the dialogue so far and the doctor's last question, work out what it is asking\nabout, and pick the item of your symptom profile to answer it with.\n\n[Symptom Profiles]\n- key: S065\n  name: Obsessions\n  description: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.\n  manifestation: I keep getting these nagging thoughts that I may have thrown away something important from my old teaching files or put a needed tool in the wrong box, and even when I tell myself it's unlikely, the thought comes back and makes it hard to settle.\n- key: S066\n  name: Compulsions\n  description: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.\n  manifestation: What brought me in is that I find myself checking the same boxes, lists, and condo drawers over and over before I can move on, even though I know perfectly well I already looked and my wife is waiting for me to just leave it be.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: Unpacking the condo has taken much longer than it should, and I’ve been putting off my library tutoring preparation and hobbies because I feel I can’t move on until things are verified enough.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 76 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S066\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Are your repetitive checks focused on preventing something specific from happening, or is it more about feeling a general sense of relief after checking?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the relief than preventing one very specific thing, if I’m being honest. I mean, there are little worries attached to it — like maybe something important is in the wrong place, or I missed something in a box or a list — but it’s not usually that I’m picturing some exact disaster happening.\n\nWhat brought me in, really, is that I’ll find myself checking the same boxes, the same lists, the same drawers in the condo, again and again before I can move on. And the frustrating part is I know I already looked. I can remember looking. But I still get this unsettled feeling, like it hasn’t quite registered, and I think, “Just check once more and then you’ll feel done.”\n\nAnd of course my wife is standing there waiting for me to just leave it be, and I’m aware of that too, which makes me feel a bit foolish. It’s not that I think checking the drawer three times is going to prevent some clear catastrophe. It’s more that if I don’t do it, I feel unfinished, like I can’t quite walk away comfortably. Then after checking, there’s a little bit of relief — sometimes only briefly — and I can move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to regain control over a situation when you repeat these checks?\n\nPatient: I suppose in a way, yes, but not control in a big dramatic sense. It’s more like I’m trying to get control over that unsettled feeling in my own head.\n\nWhat brought me in is that I find myself checking the same boxes, the same lists, and drawers in the condo over and over before I can move on. And the odd thing is, I know perfectly well I already looked. It’s not as if I blacked out or truly don’t remember. I can say to myself, “You opened that drawer already,” or “You went through that box.” But somehow it still doesn’t feel finished.\n\nAnd then my wife is there waiting for me to just leave it be, and I’m aware of how unnecessary it must look from the outside, but I still feel pulled back to it. So yes, maybe it is a kind of trying to regain control—not of the drawer or the box exactly, but of the doubt that keeps hanging around after I’ve already checked.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is more about avoiding making a mistake or making sure everything is okay?\n\nPatient: I think it’s probably both, but if I had to describe it, it’s less that I’m preventing some big obvious mistake and more that I’m trying to make sure things are okay before I can let go of it.\n\nWhat brought me in is really this pattern where I’ll check the same boxes, the same lists, the same drawers in the condo, and then somehow I’m back there checking them again. And the strange part is I know I already looked. I can remember opening the drawer or going through the box. It’s not as though I genuinely have no idea whether I did it. But it doesn’t feel settled in my mind.\n\nSo I’ll think, “Well, just look one more time and then you’ll know it’s okay.” And sometimes my wife is waiting for me to just stop and move on, and I’m very aware of that, which makes me feel a bit embarrassed. But in the moment it feels hard to just walk away, because then I’m left with that feeling that maybe I missed something or didn’t do it properly. So yes, I suppose it’s about avoiding a mistake in a small way, but mainly it’s about needing that sense that everything is okay before I can move on.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs your main concern when checking things repeatedly that you might have made a mistake, or is it more about feeling reassured that everything is in order?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s more the second one, I think — feeling reassured that everything is in order. Though I suppose there’s a little bit of “what if I made a mistake” mixed in, but that’s not the main picture in my mind.

What brought me in is that I keep checking the same boxes, the same lists, the same drawers in the condo, over and over before I can move on. And I know that sounds odd because I know perfectly well I already looked. I can remember doing it. I’ll think, “No, you already checked that drawer,” or “You already went through that list.” But it still doesn’t feel quite settled.

So I go back to it, not because I’ve found some new reason exactly, but because I’m trying to get that feeling of, all right, everything is in its place, nothing’s been missed, now I can stop. And my wife will be waiting for me to just leave it alone, which makes me feel embarrassed, honestly, because I know how it must look. But in the moment, if I don’t check again, it feels unfinished. So yes, mostly it’s about needing that reassurance that things are in order.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, thank you. What brought me in is… it’s this checking I keep doing. I’ll be at home, getting ready to leave or trying to finish something, and I find myself checking the same boxes, the same little lists, the drawers in the condo—sometimes more than once, more than twice, really—before I can let myself move on.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I know I already looked. I know it. It’s not as if I’m confused about whether I opened the drawer or checked the list. I can remember doing it, but then I feel like I have to look again anyway, just to be sure, and then sometimes that still doesn’t feel like enough. My wife will be standing there waiting for me, and I can tell she’s thinking, “Just leave it, we need to go,” and I’m thinking the same thing, frankly. But I still get pulled back to check one more time. It’s become bothersome enough that I thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any disturbing or intrusive thoughts that keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not like a voice or anything like that—it’s more of a nagging thought that keeps circling back. I’ll think, “What if I threw away something important from my old teaching files?” Or, “What if I put a tool I’m going to need into the wrong box?” And I can tell myself, very reasonably, “No, that probably didn’t happen,” or “You would have noticed,” but it doesn’t quite put the matter to rest.\n\nIt’s frustrating because the thought has a kind of stickiness to it. I can be trying to move on with my day, and then it pops back in—maybe I discarded some paper I shouldn’t have, or maybe that one tool is not where it ought to be. Even when I know it’s unlikely, it makes me unsettled, like I can’t quite relax until I’ve reassured myself somehow.\n\nDoctor: Are you checking these things because you're trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I suppose in a way, yes—but it’s not always some dramatic catastrophe I’m imagining. It’s more that I feel I can’t move on until I’ve made sure nothing has been missed or put in the wrong place. So I’ll check the same boxes, the same lists, or those condo drawers again and again, even though I know perfectly well I already looked.\n\nMy wife will be there waiting, and I can see she wants me to just leave it be, and honestly I want to leave it be too. But there’s this uncomfortable feeling that if I don’t check one more time, I might have overlooked something important. So it’s less like I truly believe something terrible is definitely going to happen, and more like I’m trying to quiet that worry enough to be able to walk away.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself checking to make sure things are symmetrical or in a specific order?\n\nPatient: Hmm… symmetry or a specific order? I don’t think so, no—not in the way I think you mean.\n\nI’m not usually looking at something and feeling it has to be perfectly lined up, or that things have to be arranged just so before I can leave it alone. It’s less about whether the objects look right or are balanced, and more… well, I suppose I’m checking whether I missed something. But if you’re asking specifically about symmetry, like making sure items are even or in a particular pattern, I haven’t really noticed that with myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to do things perfectly, or that if something is not done just right, it will bother you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I mean, I like things done properly, sure—probably most people do—but I don’t know that I have a general need for perfection, like everything has to be flawless or arranged exactly so.\n\nWhat bothers me is more the uncertainty afterward. If I’ve done something and then I start wondering whether I did it correctly, or whether I missed a step, that can bother me. But it’s not so much that I’m standing there thinking, “This has to be perfect.” It’s more like, “Did I actually do what I meant to do?” or “Am I going to regret not making sure?” So… I’d say not perfection in the strict sense. More just difficulty letting it feel settled.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried when you think about making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do—though I wouldn’t say it’s a constant panic or anything dramatic like that. It’s more of a nagging worry that starts up when I think I may have made some small mistake that could matter later.\n\nFor example, with my old teaching files, I’ll get this thought that maybe I threw away something important. Even if I can tell myself, “Well, you looked through that already,” or “It’s probably not in there,” the thought doesn’t really disappear. It sort of comes back around, and then I feel unsettled.\n\nOr with tools and boxes, I might think I put a tool in the wrong box, or that something I’ll need later has ended up somewhere it shouldn’t be. And I know, rationally, that it’s unlikely, or that I could probably sort it out later if I had to. But that doesn’t always help in the moment. The worry keeps poking at me, like I can’t quite trust that I’ve done it correctly, and then it’s hard to just leave it alone and feel finished with it.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive checks focused on preventing something specific from happening, or is it more about feeling a general sense of relief after checking?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the relief than preventing one very specific thing, if I’m being honest. I mean, there are little worries attached to it — like maybe something important is in the wrong place, or I missed something in a box or a list — but it’s not usually that I’m picturing some exact disaster happening.\n\nWhat brought me in, really, is that I’ll find myself checking the same boxes, the same lists, the same drawers in the condo, again and again before I can move on. And the frustrating part is I know I already looked. I can remember looking. But I still get this unsettled feeling, like it hasn’t quite registered, and I think, “Just check once more and then you’ll feel done.”\n\nAnd of course my wife is standing there waiting for me to just leave it be, and I’m aware of that too, which makes me feel a bit foolish. It’s not that I think checking the drawer three times is going to prevent some clear catastrophe. It’s more that if I don’t do it, I feel unfinished, like I can’t quite walk away comfortably. Then after checking, there’s a little bit of relief — sometimes only briefly — and I can move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to regain control over a situation when you repeat these checks?\n\nPatient: I suppose in a way, yes, but not control in a big dramatic sense. It’s more like I’m trying to get control over that unsettled feeling in my own head.\n\nWhat brought me in is that I find myself checking the same boxes, the same lists, and drawers in the condo over and over before I can move on. And the odd thing is, I know perfectly well I already looked. It’s not as if I blacked out or truly don’t remember. I can say to myself, “You opened that drawer already,” or “You went through that box.” But somehow it still doesn’t feel finished.\n\nAnd then my wife is there waiting for me to just leave it be, and I’m aware of how unnecessary it must look from the outside, but I still feel pulled back to it. So yes, maybe it is a kind of trying to regain control—not of the drawer or the box exactly, but of the doubt that keeps hanging around after I’ve already checked.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is more about avoiding making a mistake or making sure everything is okay?\n\nPatient: I think it’s probably both, but if I had to describe it, it’s less that I’m preventing some big obvious mistake and more that I’m trying to make sure things are okay before I can let go of it.\n\nWhat brought me in is really this pattern where I’ll check the same boxes, the same lists, the same drawers in the condo, and then somehow I’m back there checking them again. And the strange part is I know I already looked. I can remember opening the drawer or going through the box. It’s not as though I genuinely have no idea whether I did it. But it doesn’t feel settled in my mind.\n\nSo I’ll think, “Well, just look one more time and then you’ll know it’s okay.” And sometimes my wife is waiting for me to just stop and move on, and I’m very aware of that, which makes me feel a bit embarrassed. But in the moment it feels hard to just walk away, because then I’m left with that feeling that maybe I missed something or didn’t do it properly. So yes, I suppose it’s about avoiding a mistake in a small way, but mainly it’s about needing that sense that everything is okay before I can move on.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern when checking things repeatedly that you might have made a mistake, or is it more about feeling reassured that everything is in order?\n\nPatient: It’s more the second one, I think — feeling reassured that everything is in order. Though I suppose there’s a little bit of “what if I made a mistake” mixed in, but that’s not the main picture in my mind.\n\nWhat brought me in is that I keep checking the same boxes, the same lists, the same drawers in the condo, over and over before I can move on. And I know that sounds odd because I know perfectly well I already looked. I can remember doing it. I’ll think, “No, you already checked that drawer,” or “You already went through that list.” But it still doesn’t feel quite settled.\n\nSo I go back to it, not because I’ve found some new reason exactly, but because I’m trying to get that feeling of, all right, everything is in its place, nothing’s been missed, now I can stop. And my wife will be waiting for me to just leave it alone, which makes me feel embarrassed, honestly, because I know how it must look. But in the moment, if I don’t check again, it feels unfinished. So yes, mostly it’s about needing that reassurance that things are in order.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

But it’s not so much that I’m standing there thinking, “This has to be perfect.” It’s more like, “Did I actually do what I meant to do?” or “Am I going to regret not making sure?” So… I’d say not perfection in the strict sense. More just difficulty letting it feel settled.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried when you think about making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do—though I wouldn’t say it’s a constant panic or anything dramatic like that. It’s more of a nagging worry that starts up when I think I may have made some small mistake that could matter later.\n\nFor example, with my old teaching files, I’ll get this thought that maybe I threw away something important. Even if I can tell myself, “Well, you looked through that already,” or “It’s probably not in there,” the thought doesn’t really disappear. It sort of comes back around, and then I feel unsettled.\n\nOr with tools and boxes, I might think I put a tool in the wrong box, or that something I’ll need later has ended up somewhere it shouldn’t be. And I know, rationally, that it’s unlikely, or that I could probably sort it out later if I had to. But that doesn’t always help in the moment. The worry keeps poking at me, like I can’t quite trust that I’ve done it correctly, and then it’s hard to just leave it alone and feel finished with it.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive checks focused on preventing something specific from happening, or is it more about feeling a general sense of relief after checking?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the relief than preventing one very specific thing, if I’m being honest. I mean, there are little worries attached to it — like maybe something important is in the wrong place, or I missed something in a box or a list — but it’s not usually that I’m picturing some exact disaster happening.\n\nWhat brought me in, really, is that I’ll find myself checking the same boxes, the same lists, the same drawers in the condo, again and again before I can move on. And the frustrating part is I know I already looked. I can remember looking. But I still get this unsettled feeling, like it hasn’t quite registered, and I think, “Just check once more and then you’ll feel done.”\n\nAnd of course my wife is standing there waiting for me to just leave it be, and I’m aware of that too, which makes me feel a bit foolish. It’s not that I think checking the drawer three times is going to prevent some clear catastrophe. It’s more that if I don’t do it, I feel unfinished, like I can’t quite walk away comfortably. Then after checking, there’s a little bit of relief — sometimes only briefly — and I can move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to regain control over a situation when you repeat these checks?\n\nPatient: I suppose in a way, yes, but not control in a big dramatic sense. It’s more like I’m trying to get control over that unsettled feeling in my own head.\n\nWhat brought me in is that I find myself checking the same boxes, the same lists, and drawers in the condo over and over before I can move on. And the odd thing is, I know perfectly well I already looked. It’s not as if I blacked out or truly don’t remember. I can say to myself, “You opened that drawer already,” or “You went through that box.” But somehow it still doesn’t feel finished.\n\nAnd then my wife is there waiting for me to just leave it be, and I’m aware of how unnecessary it must look from the outside, but I still feel pulled back to it. So yes, maybe it is a kind of trying to regain control—not of the drawer or the box exactly, but of the doubt that keeps hanging around after I’ve already checked.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is more about avoiding making a mistake or making sure everything is okay?\n\nPatient: I think it’s probably both, but if I had to describe it, it’s less that I’m preventing some big obvious mistake and more that I’m trying to make sure things are okay before I can let go of it.\n\nWhat brought me in is really this pattern where I’ll check the same boxes, the same lists, the same drawers in the condo, and then somehow I’m back there checking them again. And the strange part is I know I already looked. I can remember opening the drawer or going through the box. It’s not as though I genuinely have no idea whether I did it. But it doesn’t feel settled in my mind.\n\nSo I’ll think, “Well, just look one more time and then you’ll know it’s okay.” And sometimes my wife is waiting for me to just stop and move on, and I’m very aware of that, which makes me feel a bit embarrassed. But in the moment it feels hard to just walk away, because then I’m left with that feeling that maybe I missed something or didn’t do it properly. So yes, I suppose it’s about avoiding a mistake in a small way, but mainly it’s about needing that sense that everything is okay before I can move on.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern when checking things repeatedly that you might have made a mistake, or is it more about feeling reassured that everything is in order?\n\nPatient: It’s more the second one, I think — feeling reassured that everything is in order. Though I suppose there’s a little bit of “what if I made a mistake” mixed in, but that’s not the main picture in my mind.\n\nWhat brought me in is that I keep checking the same boxes, the same lists, the same drawers in the condo, over and over before I can move on. And I know that sounds odd because I know perfectly well I already looked. I can remember doing it. I’ll think, “No, you already checked that drawer,” or “You already went through that list.” But it still doesn’t feel quite settled.\n\nSo I go back to it, not because I’ve found some new reason exactly, but because I’m trying to get that feeling of, all right, everything is in its place, nothing’s been missed, now I can stop. And my wife will be waiting for me to just leave it alone, which makes me feel embarrassed, honestly, because I know how it must look. But in the moment, if I don’t check again, it feels unfinished. So yes, mostly it’s about needing that reassurance that things are in order.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Yes, I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s not that I truly believe I didn’t look, because I do know I looked. I can remember opening the drawer or going through the list or checking the box. But then there’s this lingering feeling, almost like, “What if I missed one thing?” or “What if I didn’t really take it in properly?”

That’s really what brought me in. I keep finding myself checking the same boxes, the same lists, and the same condo drawers over and over before I can move on. And the frustrating part is that I know perfectly well I already looked. My wife will be there waiting for me to just leave it be, and I know she’s right, but I still feel pulled back to check again.

So yes, it’s that feeling of maybe having missed something, even when I can’t point to any real reason why I would have. It’s more of an unsettled feeling than a sensible thought.

My wife will be there waiting for me to just leave it be, and I know she’s right, but I still feel pulled back to check again.\n\nSo yes, it’s that feeling of maybe having missed something, even when I can’t point to any real reason why I would have. The worry keeps poking at me, like I can’t quite trust that I’ve done it correctly, and then it’s hard to just leave it alone and feel finished with it.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive checks focused on preventing something specific from happening, or is it more about feeling a general sense of relief after checking?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the relief than preventing one very specific thing, if I’m being honest. It’s more that I need to feel certain everything is correct, or maybe that everything is settled. I wouldn’t say I’m always picturing some disaster happening if I don’t check, at least not in a clear way. It’s more like I can’t quite move on until I’ve gotten that feeling that I looked properly and nothing is out of place.

What brought me in is exactly that: I keep checking the same boxes, the same lists, the same drawers in the condo, and I’ll do it over and over even though I know perfectly well I already looked. I can remember doing it, which is what makes it so frustrating. It’s not like I’ve genuinely forgotten.

But then I’ll still think, well, maybe just one more look, just to be sure. And my wife will be waiting for me to leave it alone, and I know she’s waiting, so I feel self-conscious about it. But if I stop before it feels right, I have this nagging sense that something isn’t correct or that I didn’t really finish checking. So yes, I’d say it’s less fear that something terrible will go wrong and more this need to feel certain before I can let it go.

My wife will be standing there waiting for me, and I can tell she’s thinking, “Just leave it, we need to go,” and I’m thinking the same thing, frankly. But I still get pulled back to check one more time. It’s become bothersome enough that I thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any disturbing or intrusive thoughts that keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not like a voice or anything like that—it’s more of a nagging thought that keeps circling back. I’ll think, “What if I threw away something important from my old teaching files?” Or, “What if I put a tool I’m going to need into the wrong box?” And I can tell myself, very reasonably, “No, that probably didn’t happen,” or “You would have noticed,” but it doesn’t quite put the matter to rest.\n\nIt’s frustrating because the thought has a kind of stickiness to it. I can be trying to move on with my day, and then it pops back in—maybe I discarded some paper I shouldn’t have, or maybe that one tool is not where it ought to be. Even when I know it’s unlikely, it makes me unsettled, like I can’t quite relax until I’ve reassured myself somehow.\n\nDoctor: Are you checking these things because you're trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I suppose in a way, yes—but it’s not always some dramatic catastrophe I’m imagining. It’s more that I feel I can’t move on until I’ve made sure nothing has been missed or put in the wrong place. So I’ll check the same boxes, the same lists, or those condo drawers again and again, even though I know perfectly well I already looked.\n\nMy wife will be there waiting, and I can see she wants me to just leave it be, and honestly I want to leave it be too. But there’s this uncomfortable feeling that if I don’t check one more time, I might have overlooked something important. So it’s less like I truly believe something terrible is definitely going to happen, and more like I’m trying to quiet that worry enough to be able to walk away.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself checking to make sure things are symmetrical or in a specific order?\n\nPatient: Hmm… symmetry or a specific order? I don’t think so, no—not in the way I think you mean.\n\nI’m not usually looking at something and feeling it has to be perfectly lined up, or that things have to be arranged just so before I can leave it alone. It’s less about whether the objects look right or are balanced, and more… well, I suppose I’m checking whether I missed something. But if you’re asking specifically about symmetry, like making sure items are even or in a particular pattern, I haven’t really noticed that with myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to do things perfectly, or that if something is not done just right, it will bother you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I mean, I like things done properly, sure—probably most people do—but I don’t know that I have a general need for perfection, like everything has to be flawless or arranged exactly so.\n\nWhat bothers me is more the uncertainty afterward. If I’ve done something and then I start wondering whether I did it correctly, or whether I missed a step, that can bother me. But it’s not so much that I’m standing there thinking, “This has to be perfect.” It’s more like, “Did I actually do what I meant to do?” or “Am I going to regret not making sure?” So… I’d say not perfection in the strict sense. More just difficulty letting it feel settled.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried when you think about making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do—though I wouldn’t say it’s a constant panic or anything dramatic like that. It’s more of a nagging worry that starts up when I think I may have made some small mistake that could matter later.\n\nFor example, with my old teaching files, I’ll get this thought that maybe I threw away something important. Even if I can tell myself, “Well, you looked through that already,” or “It’s probably not in there,” the thought doesn’t really disappear. It sort of comes back around, and then I feel unsettled.\n\nOr with tools and boxes, I might think I put a tool in the wrong box, or that something I’ll need later has ended up somewhere it shouldn’t be. And I know, rationally, that it’s unlikely, or that I could probably sort it out later if I had to. But that doesn’t always help in the moment. The worry keeps poking at me, like I can’t quite trust that I’ve done it correctly, and then it’s hard to just leave it alone and feel finished with it.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive checks focused on preventing something specific from happening, or is it more about feeling a general sense of relief after checking?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the relief than preventing one very specific thing, if I’m being honest. I mean, there are little worries attached to it — like maybe something important is in the wrong place, or I missed something in a box or a list — but it’s not usually that I’m picturing some exact disaster happening.\n\nWhat brought me in, really, is that I’ll find myself checking the same boxes, the same lists, the same drawers in the condo, again and again before I can move on. And the frustrating part is I know I already looked. I can remember looking. But I still get this unsettled feeling, like it hasn’t quite registered, and I think, “Just check once more and then you’ll feel done.”\n\nAnd of course my wife is standing there waiting for me to just leave it be, and I’m aware of that too, which makes me feel a bit foolish. It’s not that I think checking the drawer three times is going to prevent some clear catastrophe. It’s more that if I don’t do it, I feel unfinished, like I can’t quite walk away comfortably. Then after checking, there’s a little bit of relief — sometimes only briefly — and I can move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to regain control over a situation when you repeat these checks?\n\nPatient: I suppose in a way, yes, but not control in a big dramatic sense. It’s more like I’m trying to get control over that unsettled feeling in my own head.\n\nWhat brought me in is that I find myself checking the same boxes, the same lists, and drawers in the condo over and over before I can move on. And the odd thing is, I know perfectly well I already looked. It’s not as if I blacked out or truly don’t remember. I can say to myself, “You opened that drawer already,” or “You went through that box.” But somehow it still doesn’t feel finished.\n\nAnd then my wife is there waiting for me to just leave it be, and I’m aware of how unnecessary it must look from the outside, but I still feel pulled back to it. So yes, maybe it is a kind of trying to regain control—not of the drawer or the box exactly, but of the doubt that keeps hanging around after I’ve already checked.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is more about avoiding making a mistake or making sure everything is okay?\n\nPatient: I think it’s probably both, but if I had to describe it, it’s less that I’m preventing some big obvious mistake and more that I’m trying to make sure things are okay before I can let go of it.\n\nWhat brought me in is really this pattern where I’ll check the same boxes, the same lists, the same drawers in the condo, and then somehow I’m back there checking them again. And the strange part is I know I already looked. I can remember opening the drawer or going through the box. It’s not as though I genuinely have no idea whether I did it. But it doesn’t feel settled in my mind.\n\nSo I’ll think, “Well, just look one more time and then you’ll know it’s okay.” And sometimes my wife is waiting for me to just stop and move on, and I’m very aware of that, which makes me feel a bit embarrassed. But in the moment it feels hard to just walk away, because then I’m left with that feeling that maybe I missed something or didn’t do it properly. So yes, I suppose it’s about avoiding a mistake in a small way, but mainly it’s about needing that sense that everything is okay before I can move on.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern when checking things repeatedly that you might have made a mistake, or is it more about feeling reassured that everything is in order?\n\nPatient: It’s more the second one, I think — feeling reassured that everything is in order. Though I suppose there’s a little bit of “what if I made a mistake” mixed in, but that’s not the main picture in my mind.\n\nWhat brought me in is that I keep checking the same boxes, the same lists, the same drawers in the condo, over and over before I can move on. And I know that sounds odd because I know perfectly well I already looked. I can remember doing it. I’ll think, “No, you already checked that drawer,” or “You already went through that list.” But it still doesn’t feel quite settled.\n\nSo I go back to it, not because I’ve found some new reason exactly, but because I’m trying to get that feeling of, all right, everything is in its place, nothing’s been missed, now I can stop. And my wife will be waiting for me to just leave it alone, which makes me feel embarrassed, honestly, because I know how it must look. But in the moment, if I don’t check again, it feels unfinished. So yes, mostly it’s about needing that reassurance that things are in order.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling that you might have missed something what drives you to check again?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s not that I truly believe I didn’t look, because I do know I looked. I can remember opening the drawer or going through the list or checking the box. But then there’s this lingering feeling, almost like, “What if I missed one thing?” or “What if I didn’t really take it in properly?”\n\nThat’s really what brought me in. I keep finding myself checking the same boxes, the same lists, and the same condo drawers over and over before I can move on. And the frustrating part is that I know perfectly well I already looked. My wife will be there waiting for me to just leave it be, and I know she’s right, but I still feel pulled back to check again.\n\nSo yes, it’s that feeling of maybe having missed something, even when I can’t point to any real reason why I would have. It’s more of an unsettled feeling than a sensible thought.\n\nDoctor: Is the primary reason you check things multiple times because you fear something will go wrong if you don't, or because you need to feel certain everything is correct?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I need to feel certain everything is correct, or maybe that everything is settled. I wouldn’t say I’m always picturing some disaster happening if I don’t check, at least not in a clear way. It’s more like I can’t quite move on until I’ve gotten that feeling that I looked properly and nothing is out of place.\n\nWhat brought me in is exactly that: I keep checking the same boxes, the same lists, the same drawers in the condo, and I’ll do it over and over even though I know perfectly well I already looked. I can remember doing it, which is what makes it so frustrating. It’s not like I’ve genuinely forgotten.\n\nBut then I’ll still think, well, maybe just one more look, just to be sure. And my wife will be waiting for me to leave it alone, and I know she’s waiting, so I feel self-conscious about it. But if I stop before it feels right, I have this nagging sense that something isn’t correct or that I didn’t really finish checking. So yes, I’d say it’s less fear that something terrible will go wrong and more this need to feel certain before I can let it go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… yes, maybe partly, but that’s the strange thing — I often already am in control, at least in any ordinary sense. I’m the one holding the list, I’m the one opening the drawer, I’m the one looking in the box. Nobody’s rushing in and changing things on me. But it still doesn’t quite stop.

What brought me in is that I’ll check the same boxes, or the same lists, or the same drawers in the condo, and then I’ll circle back and do it again before I can move on. And I know perfectly well I already looked. I’m not confused about that. I can remember looking. But there’s still this feeling like I haven’t really settled it.

So if by “completely in control” you mean everything being calm and nothing unexpected happening, I don’t know that it would stop entirely, because it happens even when things are calm. My wife might just be standing there waiting for me to leave it be, and I know she’s right, and I know I’ve already checked, but I still feel this pull to look again before I can let it go.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, thank you. What brought me in is… it’s this checking I keep doing. I’ll be at home, getting ready to leave or trying to finish something, and I find myself checking the same boxes, the same little lists, the drawers in the condo—sometimes more than once, more than twice, really—before I can let myself move on.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I know I already looked. I know it. It’s not as if I’m confused about whether I opened the drawer or checked the list. I can remember doing it, but then I feel like I have to look again anyway, just to be sure, and then sometimes that still doesn’t feel like enough. My wife will be standing there waiting for me, and I can tell she’s thinking, “Just leave it, we need to go,” and I’m thinking the same thing, frankly. But I still get pulled back to check one more time. It’s become bothersome enough that I thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any disturbing or intrusive thoughts that keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not like a voice or anything like that—it’s more of a nagging thought that keeps circling back. I’ll think, “What if I threw away something important from my old teaching files?” Or, “What if I put a tool I’m going to need into the wrong box?” And I can tell myself, very reasonably, “No, that probably didn’t happen,” or “You would have noticed,” but it doesn’t quite put the matter to rest.\n\nIt’s frustrating because the thought has a kind of stickiness to it. I can be trying to move on with my day, and then it pops back in—maybe I discarded some paper I shouldn’t have, or maybe that one tool is not where it ought to be. Even when I know it’s unlikely, it makes me unsettled, like I can’t quite relax until I’ve reassured myself somehow.\n\nDoctor: Are you checking these things because you're trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I suppose in a way, yes—but it’s not always some dramatic catastrophe I’m imagining. It’s more that I feel I can’t move on until I’ve made sure nothing has been missed or put in the wrong place. So I’ll check the same boxes, the same lists, or those condo drawers again and again, even though I know perfectly well I already looked.\n\nMy wife will be there waiting, and I can see she wants me to just leave it be, and honestly I want to leave it be too. But there’s this uncomfortable feeling that if I don’t check one more time, I might have overlooked something important. So it’s less like I truly believe something terrible is definitely going to happen, and more like I’m trying to quiet that worry enough to be able to walk away.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself checking to make sure things are symmetrical or in a specific order?\n\nPatient: Hmm… symmetry or a specific order? I don’t think so, no—not in the way I think you mean.\n\nI’m not usually looking at something and feeling it has to be perfectly lined up, or that things have to be arranged just so before I can leave it alone. It’s less about whether the objects look right or are balanced, and more… well, I suppose I’m checking whether I missed something. But if you’re asking specifically about symmetry, like making sure items are even or in a particular pattern, I haven’t really noticed that with myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to do things perfectly, or that if something is not done just right, it will bother you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I mean, I like things done properly, sure—probably most people do—but I don’t know that I have a general need for perfection, like everything has to be flawless or arranged exactly so.\n\nWhat bothers me is more the uncertainty afterward. If I’ve done something and then I start wondering whether I did it correctly, or whether I missed a step, that can bother me. But it’s not so much that I’m standing there thinking, “This has to be perfect.” It’s more like, “Did I actually do what I meant to do?” or “Am I going to regret not making sure?” So… I’d say not perfection in the strict sense. More just difficulty letting it feel settled.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried when you think about making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do—though I wouldn’t say it’s a constant panic or anything dramatic like that. It’s more of a nagging worry that starts up when I think I may have made some small mistake that could matter later.\n\nFor example, with my old teaching files, I’ll get this thought that maybe I threw away something important. Even if I can tell myself, “Well, you looked through that already,” or “It’s probably not in there,” the thought doesn’t really disappear. It sort of comes back around, and then I feel unsettled.\n\nOr with tools and boxes, I might think I put a tool in the wrong box, or that something I’ll need later has ended up somewhere it shouldn’t be. And I know, rationally, that it’s unlikely, or that I could probably sort it out later if I had to. But that doesn’t always help in the moment. The worry keeps poking at me, like I can’t quite trust that I’ve done it correctly, and then it’s hard to just leave it alone and feel finished with it.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive checks focused on preventing something specific from happening, or is it more about feeling a general sense of relief after checking?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the relief than preventing one very specific thing, if I’m being honest. I mean, there are little worries attached to it — like maybe something important is in the wrong place, or I missed something in a box or a list — but it’s not usually that I’m picturing some exact disaster happening.\n\nWhat brought me in, really, is that I’ll find myself checking the same boxes, the same lists, the same drawers in the condo, again and again before I can move on. And the frustrating part is I know I already looked. I can remember looking. But I still get this unsettled feeling, like it hasn’t quite registered, and I think, “Just check once more and then you’ll feel done.”\n\nAnd of course my wife is standing there waiting for me to just leave it be, and I’m aware of that too, which makes me feel a bit foolish. It’s not that I think checking the drawer three times is going to prevent some clear catastrophe. It’s more that if I don’t do it, I feel unfinished, like I can’t quite walk away comfortably. Then after checking, there’s a little bit of relief — sometimes only briefly — and I can move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to regain control over a situation when you repeat these checks?\n\nPatient: I suppose in a way, yes, but not control in a big dramatic sense. It’s more like I’m trying to get control over that unsettled feeling in my own head.\n\nWhat brought me in is that I find myself checking the same boxes, the same lists, and drawers in the condo over and over before I can move on. And the odd thing is, I know perfectly well I already looked. It’s not as if I blacked out or truly don’t remember. I can say to myself, “You opened that drawer already,” or “You went through that box.” But somehow it still doesn’t feel finished.\n\nAnd then my wife is there waiting for me to just leave it be, and I’m aware of how unnecessary it must look from the outside, but I still feel pulled back to it. So yes, maybe it is a kind of trying to regain control—not of the drawer or the box exactly, but of the doubt that keeps hanging around after I’ve already checked.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is more about avoiding making a mistake or making sure everything is okay?\n\nPatient: I think it’s probably both, but if I had to describe it, it’s less that I’m preventing some big obvious mistake and more that I’m trying to make sure things are okay before I can let go of it.\n\nWhat brought me in is really this pattern where I’ll check the same boxes, the same lists, the same drawers in the condo, and then somehow I’m back there checking them again. And the strange part is I know I already looked. I can remember opening the drawer or going through the box. It’s not as though I genuinely have no idea whether I did it. But it doesn’t feel settled in my mind.\n\nSo I’ll think, “Well, just look one more time and then you’ll know it’s okay.” And sometimes my wife is waiting for me to just stop and move on, and I’m very aware of that, which makes me feel a bit embarrassed. But in the moment it feels hard to just walk away, because then I’m left with that feeling that maybe I missed something or didn’t do it properly. So yes, I suppose it’s about avoiding a mistake in a small way, but mainly it’s about needing that sense that everything is okay before I can move on.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern when checking things repeatedly that you might have made a mistake, or is it more about feeling reassured that everything is in order?\n\nPatient: It’s more the second one, I think — feeling reassured that everything is in order. Though I suppose there’s a little bit of “what if I made a mistake” mixed in, but that’s not the main picture in my mind.\n\nWhat brought me in is that I keep checking the same boxes, the same lists, the same drawers in the condo, over and over before I can move on. And I know that sounds odd because I know perfectly well I already looked. I can remember doing it. I’ll think, “No, you already checked that drawer,” or “You already went through that list.” But it still doesn’t feel quite settled.\n\nSo I go back to it, not because I’ve found some new reason exactly, but because I’m trying to get that feeling of, all right, everything is in its place, nothing’s been missed, now I can stop. And my wife will be waiting for me to just leave it alone, which makes me feel embarrassed, honestly, because I know how it must look. But in the moment, if I don’t check again, it feels unfinished. So yes, mostly it’s about needing that reassurance that things are in order.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling that you might have missed something what drives you to check again?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s not that I truly believe I didn’t look, because I do know I looked. I can remember opening the drawer or going through the list or checking the box. But then there’s this lingering feeling, almost like, “What if I missed one thing?” or “What if I didn’t really take it in properly?”\n\nThat’s really what brought me in. I keep finding myself checking the same boxes, the same lists, and the same condo drawers over and over before I can move on. And the frustrating part is that I know perfectly well I already looked. My wife will be there waiting for me to just leave it be, and I know she’s right, but I still feel pulled back to check again.\n\nSo yes, it’s that feeling of maybe having missed something, even when I can’t point to any real reason why I would have. It’s more of an unsettled feeling than a sensible thought.\n\nDoctor: Is the primary reason you check things multiple times because you fear something will go wrong if you don't, or because you need to feel certain everything is correct?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I need to feel certain everything is correct, or maybe that everything is settled. I wouldn’t say I’m always picturing some disaster happening if I don’t check, at least not in a clear way. It’s more like I can’t quite move on until I’ve gotten that feeling that I looked properly and nothing is out of place.\n\nWhat brought me in is exactly that: I keep checking the same boxes, the same lists, the same drawers in the condo, and I’ll do it over and over even though I know perfectly well I already looked. And my wife will be waiting for me to leave it alone, and I know she’s waiting, so I feel self-conscious about it. But if I stop before it feels right, I have this nagging sense that something isn’t correct or that I didn’t really finish checking. So yes, I’d say it’s less fear that something terrible will go wrong and more this need to feel certain before I can let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking would stop if you felt completely in control of the situation?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, maybe partly, but that’s the strange thing — I often already am in control, at least in any ordinary sense.
